The Ghosts of Empires: Extracting Modularity from Interleaving-Based Proofs (Extended Version) CCS: Theory of computation Logic and verification CCS: Theory of computation Concurrency CCS: Theory of computation Program verification
Frank Schüssele, Matthias Zumkeller, Miriam Lagunes-Rochin, Dominik Klumpp

Abstract
. Implementation bugs threaten the soundness of algorithmic software verifiers. Generating correctness certificates for correct programs allows for efficient independent validation of verification results, and thus helps to reveal such bugs. Automatic generation of small, compact correctness proofs for concurrent programs is challenging, as the correctness arguments may depend on the particular interleaving, which can lead to exponential explosion. We present an approach that converts an interleaving-based correctness proof, as generated by many algorithmic verifiers, into a thread-modular correctness proof in the style of Owicki and Gries. We automatically synthesize ghost variables that capture the relevant interleaving information, and abstract away irrelevant details. Our evaluation shows that the approach is efficient in practice and generates compact proofs, compared to a baseline.



1.
Introduction

Formal verification promises to eliminate certain kinds of defects, and to ensure correctness of verified systems with
absolute, mathematical certainty
.
Yet, to deliver on these promises,
in practice we rely on implementations of such formal approaches in verification
tools
.
Though based on sound methodology,
verification tools (that are sophisticated enough to handle real-world applications)
are themselves complex software systems,
and are prone to subtle implementation bugs.
To address this issue and increase the trustworthiness of formal methods tools generally,
there is an increasing movement towards requiring such tools to accompany their output
(e.g., a verdict of
correct
or
incorrect
) with an additional certificate
that allows a user to efficiently check the accuracy of the output (i.e., to
validate
the certificate).
Such certificates, sometimes also called
proofs
or
witnesses
,
have long become standard practice for propositional SAT solvers
(
Wetzler et al. 2014
)
,
and are a topic of interest also in hardware model checking
(
Froleyks et al. 2025
;
Froleyks et al. 2024
)
,
satisfiability modulo theories (SMT)
(
Hoenicke and Schindler 2022
;
Schurr et al. 2021
)
,
classical planning
(
Eriksson et al. 2017
)
,
and (as in this work) software verification
(
Ayaziová et al. 2024
;
Beyer and Strejcek 2022
;
Erhard et al. 2025
)
.

We consider in particular correctness proofs
for the algorithmic verification of concurrent programs.
While generating and checking correctness proofs for sequential programs is already difficult,
in particular for real-world programming languages
(
Ayaziová et al. 2024
;
Beyer and Strejcek 2022
)
,
correctness proofs for concurrent programs present an even greater challenge.
As correctness must be established for all possible interleavings of a concurrent program,
a naïve approach to correctness proofs falls victim to the
interleaving problem
:
the proof is typically exponentially large in the size of the program.
This explosion of size runs counter to the idea of certificates,
as validating such proofs is prohibitively expensive.
Therefore, we present an approach to convert the internal, interleaving-based proofs produced by many algorithmic verifiers into compact
thread-modular
proofs for the verified concurrent programs.
Rather than explicitly encoding all interleaving information in a single global proof, thread-modular proofs instead reason over each thread locally.
Where necessary, interleaving information can be encoded through the use of
ghost variables
.
Our algorithm detects and takes advantage of modularity in the given interleaving-based proof,
to encode only as little interleaving information as needed.
The generated compact correctness proofs can be efficiently validated,
and thereby
enable testing the consistency of a verifier’s internal reasoning,
revealing implementation bugs or increasing trust in the verifier’s soundness.
The key advantage is that no ground truth regarding the correctness of the analysed program is required.
Moreover, validation can increase the confidence in the correctness of a particular, highly-critical piece of software.

Since the seminal work of
Owicki and Gries 1976

(
Owicki and Gries 1976
)
,
there has been a wealth of research into suitable program logics for correctness proofs of concurrent programs.
This includes,
among others, the
rely-guarantee
approach
(
Jones 1981
)
,
and many works building on
concurrent separation logics

(
O’Hearn 2004
;
Brookes 2007
;
Brookes and O’Hearn 2016
)
.
These works have typically targeted interactive or deductive verification.
By contrast, our work targets algorithmic verification.
Yet, we do not propose a new verification algorithm:
Instead, our approach is compatible with many existing verification algorithms (and mature tools implementing these algorithms)
that explicitly consider all the possible interleavings of a concurrent program.
We propose to analyse the resulting interleaving-based proof,
and construct from it a more compact correctness proof
specifically for the purpose of exporting a certificate.
We base our approach on the work of
Owicki and Gries 1976

(
Owicki and Gries 1976
)
,
as the given proof rule is conceptually simple yet complete.
The key challenge addressed by our approach is to identify which interleaving information is required for the correctness argument,
and which information can be abstracted away.
In this sense, our approach differs from classical constructions of Owicki-Gries proofs from interleaving-based proofs,
as employed in completeness arguments
(
Nieto 2001
)
.
Such constructions typically capture an excessive amount of interleaving information (e.g. the entire history, or the entire global control state), and are not intended for practical use.
Our approach operates on a generic representation of interleaving-based proofs that can capture classical techniques such as abstract interpretation
(
Cousot and Cousot 1977
)
, predicate abstraction
(
Graf and Saïdi 1997
)
, Impact
(
McMillan 2006
)
or trace abstraction
(
Heizmann et al. 2009
)
.

Figure 1

Example Program

Example.

Consider the example program in
Fig.

1
.
It begins with the assumption that the variable
x
is positive.
Then, two threads run in parallel.
The left-hand thread performs a computation on its local variable
y
,
and finally asserts that
y<x
holds.
The right-hand thread performs a computation on its local variable
z
.
After their respective computations, each thread increments the variable
x
.
When both threads have terminated, the program asserts that
x>2
.
An interleaving-based verifier might first show that the local assert in the left-hand thread never fails, using the inductive assertions
\top
(initially),
y\leq x
(once
y
has been initialized to
x
), and
y<x
(once
x
has been incremented by the thread).
Then, the verifier might show that the assert statement
x>2
holds, using the inductive assertions
\top
(initially),
x>1
(after either of the two increments), and
x>2
(after the second increment).
Our goal is to construct an Owicki-Gries proof from these assertions.
I.e., we annotate every line
\ell_{i}
with a formula
\omega(\ell_{i})
,
such that the annotation is inductive within each thread,
and executing statements in the other thread is guaranteed to preserve the formula (interference-freedom).

However, this is not always possible.
In particular, to show the final assert
x>2
,
the annotation at
\ell_{12}
needs to distinguish whether the left-hand thread has already incremented
x
(in which case,
x>1
holds) or not (in which case, we only know that
x>0
).
Without this case distinction, it is not possible to give an Owicki-Gries proof for
x>2

(
Apt et al. 2009
)
.
Hence,
Owicki and Gries 1976
allow introducing
ghost variables
that are updated throughout the program, but do not otherwise influence the execution.
Such ghost variables can record information about the executed interleaving (such as, whether the other thread has incremented
x
already), which can then be used in the proof.
In our case, we introduce two boolean ghost variables

.
Analogously, after the increment in line
\ell_{12}
has been executed,
the formula

We return to this example program throughout the paper to demonstrate our approach.

Related Work.

Beyond the aforementioned works in deductive and interactive verification,
thread-modular correctness proofs for concurrent programs have also been studied in algorithmic verification.
All of the works below differ from our approach in that they present specialized verification algorithms,
rather than extracting thread-modularity from a proof after verification has completed.

Gupta et al. 2011

(
Gupta et al. 2011
)
present an abstraction refinement approach that verifies programs using a rely-guarantee method.
I.e., the approach uses transition predicate abstraction to over-approximate the transitions of environment threads.
They allow the proof of a thread to use interleaving information in the form of control locations and local variables of other threads,
but bias the method to first try finding a solution without any such interleaving information.

Miné 2012

(
Miné 2012
)
introduces thread-modular abstract interpretation,
also following a rely-guarantee scheme.
In later works
(
Miné 2014
;
Monat and Miné 2017
)
,
this is extended to
flow-sensitive
analyses,
which allow for case distinctions based on the locations of other threads.
They group control locations into
abstract control locations
,
which resembles our abstraction of interleaving information.
Here, the abstraction is an input parameter of the analysis, whereas we derive it automatically.
The thread-modular abstract interpreter
Goblint

(
Saan et al. 2023
;
Vojdani et al. 2016
)
is similarly based on thread-modular abstract interpretation.

In verification of programs with an unbounded number of threads (parameterized programs),
thread-modularity has been relaxed and generalized to
thread-modularity at level
k

(
Hoenicke et al. 2017
)
.
Rather than proving correctness of individual threads,
these methods instead associate invariants to
k
-tuples of control locations from different threads.
Several works have studied constrained Horn-clause systems to derive such proofs
(
Grebenshchikov et al. 2012
;
Hojjat et al. 2014
;
Gurfinkel et al. 2016
)
.
Here, the local states of additional threads can be seen to serve as a particular kind of ghost state.
However, resulting the proof rule remains incomplete.

Finally, previous work has also considered the synthesis of ghost variables not based on capturing the state of other threads.
For instance,
Farzan et al. 2014

(
Farzan et al. 2014
)
develop an abstraction-refinement approach that integrates the synthesis of ghost
counter
variables.

Contributions and Roadmap.

The remainder of the paper is organized as follows.

Section

2
defines our formal model of concurrent programs.
In
Section

3
, we present a general notion of interleaving-based proofs and an adaptation of Owicki-Gries proofs to our formal setting; thereby defining input and output of our approach.
The subsequent four sections contain our core technical contributions:

•

In
Section

4
, we introduce
empires
, an abstract representation of the interleaving information relevant to a proof,
and show how to construct Owicki-Gries proofs from empires.

•

Section

5
presents our algorithm to automatically extract such an empire from an interleaving-based proof,
abstracting away irrelevant interleaving information.

•

We refine the approach in
Section

6
,
with a criterion to identify which components of a modular interleaving-based proof are relevant for particular threads.

•

The evaluation in
Section

7
demonstrates the practicability of our approach and the quality of the generated Owicki-Gries proofs, compared to a baseline.

We conclude with a discussion and an outlook to future work (
Section

8
).

2.
Background: Concurrent Programs

Our model of concurrent programs uses Petri nets to represent the possible control flow.
Note that typical concurrent programs, such as those based on POSIX threads (pthreads), can be transformed to such a Petri net-based model
(
Heizmann et al. 2024
)
.
In
Section

8
, we discuss in more detail how our approach also applies to verifiers whose internal program model does not rely on Petri nets.

Definition 2.1.

A
Petri program
is a tuple
\mathcal{P}=(\Sigma,P,T,F,m_{\mathrm{init}},\lambda,P_{\mathsf{err}})
with a finite alphabet
\Sigma
of statements,
a finite set of
places

P
,
a finite set of
transitions

T
with
P\cap T=\emptyset
,
a
flow relation

F\subseteq(P\times T)\cup(T\times P)
,
an
initial marking

m_{\mathrm{init}}:P\to\mathbb{N}
,
a
labeling
which maps transitions to statements
\lambda:T\to\Sigma
,
and a set of
error places

P_{\mathsf{err}}\subseteq P
.

p_{0}

x>0

p_{1}

y:=x

p_{2}

y>0

y:=y-1

p_{3}

x:=x+1

p_{4}

y>=x

p_{5}

x!=0

z:=z/x

p_{6}

x:=x+1

p_{7}

x<3

e_{1}
e_{2}

Figure 2
.
Petri program corresponding to
Fig.

1
. Transitions are drawn as black boxes, places as circles. Double circles indicate error places. Arrows represent the flow relation. Initially, only
p_{0}
has a token (black dot).

Each statement in
\Sigma
consists of guards and assignments over program variables, and is executed atomically.

V_{\mathcal{P}}
denotes the set of all program variables occurring in statements of
\mathcal{P}
.

Figure

2
presents the program from
Fig.

1
as a Petri program in a graphical form.

Concurrent Control Flow.

The control flow of the program follows the Petri net semantics.
Specifically, each control state of the program is represented by a
marking
,
i.e., a map
m:P\to\mathbb{N}
that assigns to each place the number of
tokens
that are currently in the place.
The set of predecessor places
{}^{\bullet}{t}
of a transition
t\in T
consists of all places
p\in P
such that
(p,t)\in F
holds.
Similarly, the set of successors
{t}^{\bullet}
consists of all places
p
such that
(t,p)\in F
holds.
When all predecessor places of a transition contain at least one token, we say that the transition is
enabled
in the current marking.
The transition can then be
fired
, resulting in a new marking.

Definition 2.2.

A transition
t\in T
is
enabled
in a marking
m
if each place
p\in{}^{\bullet}{t}
contains a token (
m(p)>0
).
Firing the transition results in the successor marking
m^{\prime}
, denoted
m\mathrel{\triangleright_{t}}m^{\prime}
, if for each
p\in P
we have

m^{\prime}(p)=m(p)-1
if
p\in{}^{\bullet}{t}
and
p\notin{t}^{\bullet}
,

m^{\prime}(p)=m(p)+1
if
p\in{t}^{\bullet}
and
p\notin{}^{\bullet}{t}
,
and
m^{\prime}(p)=m(p)
otherwise.

A sequence
m_{0}\mathrel{\triangleright_{t_{1}}}m_{1}\mathrel{\triangleright_{t_{2}}}\dots\mathrel{\triangleright_{t_{n}}}m_{n}
beginning at the initial marking
m_{0}=m_{\mathrm{init}}
is called a
firing sequence
.
A marking
m
is
reachable
if there exists a firing sequence
m_{0}\mathrel{\triangleright_{t_{1}}}m_{1}\mathrel{\triangleright_{t_{2}}}\dots\mathrel{\triangleright_{t_{n}}}m_{n}
such that
m_{n}=m
.
We write
Reach
for the set of all reachable markings.

We consider only
one-safe
Petri programs, i.e., Petri programs where in all reachable markings, every place has at most one token.
To simplify notation, we identify each reachable marking
m:P\to\{0,1\}
with the set
\{\,p\mid m(p)=1\,\}
of all places that have a token.
The firing relation for reachable markings then simplifies:
m\mathrel{\triangleright_{t}}m^{\prime}
holds if and only if
{}^{\bullet}{t}\subseteq m
and
m^{\prime}=(m\setminus{}^{\bullet}{t})\cup{t}^{\bullet}
.
We further assume that every transition in the Petri program is enabled in some reachable marking.
1
1

1

This assumption simplifies the technical details of the presentation.
Removing transitions that can never be enabled does not affect correctness of the Petri program.
If desired, an Owicki-Gries proof of the full Petri program can be easily constructed from a proof of the program where such transitions have been removed.

While we sometimes refer to “threads” on an intuitive level,
Petri programs have no such concept.
To formalize key conditions for thread-modular proofs,
such as two places (or transitions) “belonging to different threads”,
we instead rely on the following definitions:

Definition 2.3.

Two places
p,p^{\prime}\in P
are
co-related
, denoted as
{p}\mathrel{\mathsf{co}}{p^{\prime}}
, if
p\neq p^{\prime}
and there exists a reachable marking
m
such that
p\in m
and
p^{\prime}\in m
.
A place
p
and a transition
t
are
co-marked
if
p\notin{}^{\bullet}{t}
and there exists a reachable marking
m
such that
p\in m
and
t
is enabled in
m
(i.e.,
{}^{\bullet}{t}\subseteq m
).

Correctness.

Informally speaking, a Petri program is correct if it cannot feasibly reach an error place.
To formalize this, we use the notion of
Hoare triples
.

For a given set of variables
V
, we write
\mathbf{Fm}(V)
for the set of all first-order formulas whose free variables are included in
V
.
Given assertions
\varphi
and
\psi
over variables
V
, i.e.,
\varphi,\psi\in\mathbf{Fm}(V)
, and a statement
s\!t
that refers only to variables in
V
,
a
Hoare triple
has the form
{\left\{\varphi\middle\}\;s\!t\;\middle\{\psi\right\}}
.
The formulas
\varphi
and
\psi
are referred to as precondition resp. postcondition.
The Hoare triple
holds
if, whenever
\varphi
holds and
s\!t
is executed and terminates, the assertion
\psi
must hold afterwards.
We extend this notion to sequences of statements.
Note in particular that the Hoare triple
{\left\{\top\middle\}\;s\!t_{1}\ldots s\!t_{n}\;\middle\{\bot\right\}}
holds
if and only if the sequence
s\!t_{1}\ldots s\!t_{n}
cannot be executed
(because the guard of one of the statements cannot be satisfied, and the statement blocks the execution).

Definition 2.4.

A Petri program
\mathcal{P}=(\Sigma,P,T,F,m_{\mathrm{init}},\lambda,P_{\mathsf{err}})
is
correct
if for all firing sequences
m_{0}\mathrel{\triangleright_{t_{1}}}\dots\mathrel{\triangleright_{t_{n}}}m_{n}
with
m_{n}\cap P_{\mathsf{err}}\neq\emptyset
,
the Hoare triple
{\left\{\top\middle\}\;\lambda(t_{1})\dots\lambda(t_{n})\;\middle\{\bot\right\}}
holds.

3.
Interleaving-Based vs. Thread-Modular Correctness Proofs

Correctness proofs of concurrent programs take different forms.

Interleaving-based proofs
operate on the interleavings of all concurrent threads, and do not separate local states of different threads from each other or from the global state.
By contrast,
thread-modular proofs
establish correctness in a thread-by-thread fashion, and minimize the required information about global state and interference by other threads.
In the following, we define a general notion of interleaving-based proofs, which we call
invariant domains
, characterizing the kind of proof our approach takes as input.
We then discuss
Owicki-Gries annotations
, the thread-modular proof format produced by our approach.

3.1.
Interleaving-Based Correctness Proofs

The approach presented in this paper works on correctness proofs for concurrent programs generated by verification algorithms that prove correctness for all program interleavings.
To capture a general notion of such interleaving-based proofs, we introduce
invariant domains
.

Definition 3.1.

An
invariant domain
for
\mathcal{P}
is a tuple
(A,\mathit{post})

consisting of a finite set
A\subseteq\mathbf{Fm}(V_{\mathcal{P}})
with
\bot,\top\in A

and a function
\mathit{post}:A\times T\rightarrow A

such that for all
\varphi\in A
and
t\in T
,
the Hoare triple
{\left\{\varphi\middle\}\;\lambda(t)\;\middle\{\mathit{post}\left(\varphi,t\right)\right\}}
holds.

For an invariant domain
(A,\mathit{post})
,
the set of
reachable abstract configurations

\textsc{ReachConf}(A,\mathit{post})

is the smallest subset of
\textsc{Reach}\times A
that contains
\langle m_{\mathrm{init}},\top\rangle
,
such that for every transition
t\in T
and for each
\langle m,\varphi\rangle\in\textsc{ReachConf}(A,\mathit{post})
,
where
t
is enabled in
m
and
m\mathrel{\triangleright_{t}}m^{\prime}
,
it also holds that
\langle m^{\prime},\mathit{post}\left(\varphi,t\right)\rangle\in\textsc{ReachConf}(A,\mathit{post})
.

Definition 3.2.

An invariant domain
(A,\mathit{post})
for
\mathcal{P}
is
safe
if for every reachable abstract configuration
\langle m,\varphi\rangle\in\textsc{ReachConf}(A,\mathit{post})
with
m\cap P_{\mathsf{err}}\neq\emptyset
,
it holds that
\varphi
is equal to
\bot
.

Safe invariant domains for Petri programs can be computed with many classical algorithmic verification techniques.
For instance, when verifying a program with
abstract interpretation

(
Cousot and Cousot 1977
)
,
an invariant domain can be extracted from the fixpoint computed by the analysis.
2
2

2

The attentive reader may notice that an invariant domain is itself a special case of an abstract domain.
We introduce the distinct terminology in part due to the special conditions (such as finiteness),
and in part to avoid confusing the invariant domain derived by applying e.g. interval analysis on a program (which consists of finitely many intervals, and a program-specific
\mathit{post}
)
with the domain used during the analysis itself (here, the interval domain).

A safe
predicate abstraction

B

(
Graf and Saïdi 1997
)
, as found by
predicate abstraction refinement

(
Ball and Rajamani 2001
)
,
induces a safe invariant domain
(A,\mathit{post}_{B})
,
with
A=\{\,\bigwedge B^{\prime}\mid B^{\prime}\subseteq B\,\}
the set of all conjunctions over
B

and
\mathit{post}_{B}
the standard post-operator of predicate abstraction
(i.e.,
\mathit{post}_{B}(\bigwedge B^{\prime},s\!t)=\bigwedge\big\{\,\psi\in B\mid{\left\{\bigwedge B^{\prime}\middle\}\;s\!t\;\middle\{\psi\right\}}\text{ holds}\,\big\}
).
Invariant domains can similarly be derived from the abstract reachability graph constructed by the
Impact
algorithm
(
McMillan 2006
)
.
In
trace abstraction refinement

(
Heizmann et al. 2009
)
,
the product of the computed
Floyd-Hoare automata
also allows to define a safe invariant domain.
While some of these techniques have been primarily applied for sequential programs,
they extend to concurrent programs as well,
either by considering a “naïve sequentialization” derived from the Petri program’s reachability graph,
or through adaptations
(
Dietsch et al. 2021
)
that help to avoid the state space explosion problem.

Proposition 3.3.

If there exists a safe invariant domain for
\mathcal{P}
, then
\mathcal{P}
is correct.

3.2.
Owicki-Gries Annotations

This section introduces
Owicki-Gries annotations
, the format of correctness proofs for concurrent programs that our approach computes.
As the name implies, these annotations are based on the principles laid out by
Owicki and Gries 1976

(
Owicki and Gries 1976
)
.
They are thread-modular, in the sense that each thread of a concurrent program is given its own correctness proof, for the most part ignoring the local states of other threads.
Where necessary, limited information about these local states can be captured by the introduction of auxiliary
ghost variables
that record relevant information about the executed interleaving,
do not influence the program’s own computation, and can be referred to in the formulae of the proofs of each thread.
This confinement of information about local states of other threads and the executed interleaving to only the relevant aspects
allows Owicki-Gries style proofs (in most cases) to remain concise and efficiently checkable,
while still forming a complete proof system
(
Owicki 1976
;
Lamport 1977
)
.
Whereas
Owicki and Gries 1976
present their proof system as a Hoare-style calculus on the program syntax,
we adapt it here to a more control flow-oriented view,
which is more suitable for algorithmic verification tools.
Therefore, we define
Owicki-Gries annotations
for our notion of Petri programs.
For this purpose, and for the remainder of the paper, we always assume a fixed Petri program
\mathcal{P}=(\Sigma,P,T,F,m_{\mathrm{init}},\lambda,P_{\mathsf{err}})
.

Definition 3.4.

An
Owicki-Gries annotation
of
\mathcal{P}
is a
tuple
\mathcal{O\!G}=(V_{\mathord{\mathchoice{\vrule width=0.0pt,height=0.0pt,depth=1.22917pt\picture(3.2,2.6)(-1.6,-1.0)\roundcap\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1,-1)(-1,-.5)(-1,0)\Line(-1,0)(-1,.5)\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1,-1)(1,-.5)(1,0)\Line(1,0)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1,.5)(-1,2)(1,2)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1.1,-1.1)(-.7,-1.9)(-.3,-1.5)\qbezier{}{-0.3}{-1.5}{0}{-1.2}{0.3}{-1.5}\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1.1,-1.1)(.7,-1.9)(.3,-1.5)\put(-0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\put(0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\endpicture}{\vrule width=0.0pt,height=0.0pt,depth=1.22917pt\picture(3.2,2.6)(-1.6,-1.0)\roundcap\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1,-1)(-1,-.5)(-1,0)\Line(-1,0)(-1,.5)\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1,-1)(1,-.5)(1,0)\Line(1,0)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1,.5)(-1,2)(1,2)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1.1,-1.1)(-.7,-1.9)(-.3,-1.5)\qbezier{}{-0.3}{-1.5}{0}{-1.2}{0.3}{-1.5}\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1.1,-1.1)(.7,-1.9)(.3,-1.5)\put(-0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\put(0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\endpicture}{\vrule width=0.0pt,height=0.0pt,depth=1.22917pt\picture(3.2,2.6)(-1.6,-1.0)\roundcap\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1,-1)(-1,-.5)(-1,0)\Line(-1,0)(-1,.5)\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1,-1)(1,-.5)(1,0)\Line(1,0)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1,.5)(-1,2)(1,2)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1.1,-1.1)(-.7,-1.9)(-.3,-1.5)\qbezier{}{-0.3}{-1.5}{0}{-1.2}{0.3}{-1.5}\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1.1,-1.1)(.7,-1.9)(.3,-1.5)\put(-0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\put(0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\endpicture}{\vrule width=0.0pt,height=0.0pt,depth=1.22917pt\picture(3.2,2.6)(-1.6,-1.0)\roundcap\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1,-1)(-1,-.5)(-1,0)\Line(-1,0)(-1,.5)\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1,-1)(1,-.5)(1,0)\Line(1,0)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1,.5)(-1,2)(1,2)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1.1,-1.1)(-.7,-1.9)(-.3,-1.5)\qbezier{}{-0.3}{-1.5}{0}{-1.2}{0.3}{-1.5}\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1.1,-1.1)(.7,-1.9)(.3,-1.5)\put(-0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\put(0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\endpicture}}},\omega,\gamma,\rho)
, such that:

Each ghost variable has a (possibly infinite) domain.

•

When executing such ghost updates, all these expressions are evaluated,
and then simultaneously assigned to the respective ghost variables.
If
\gamma(t)
is the empty partial function, all ghost variables remain unchanged.

For an Owicki-Gries annotation, we also need to define what it means to be

.

Definition 3.5.

An Owicki-Gries annotation
\mathcal{O\!G}=(V_{\mathord{\mathchoice{\vrule width=0.0pt,height=0.0pt,depth=1.22917pt\picture(3.2,2.6)(-1.6,-1.0)\roundcap\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1,-1)(-1,-.5)(-1,0)\Line(-1,0)(-1,.5)\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1,-1)(1,-.5)(1,0)\Line(1,0)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1,.5)(-1,2)(1,2)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1.1,-1.1)(-.7,-1.9)(-.3,-1.5)\qbezier{}{-0.3}{-1.5}{0}{-1.2}{0.3}{-1.5}\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1.1,-1.1)(.7,-1.9)(.3,-1.5)\put(-0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\put(0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\endpicture}{\vrule width=0.0pt,height=0.0pt,depth=1.22917pt\picture(3.2,2.6)(-1.6,-1.0)\roundcap\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1,-1)(-1,-.5)(-1,0)\Line(-1,0)(-1,.5)\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1,-1)(1,-.5)(1,0)\Line(1,0)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1,.5)(-1,2)(1,2)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1.1,-1.1)(-.7,-1.9)(-.3,-1.5)\qbezier{}{-0.3}{-1.5}{0}{-1.2}{0.3}{-1.5}\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1.1,-1.1)(.7,-1.9)(.3,-1.5)\put(-0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\put(0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\endpicture}{\vrule width=0.0pt,height=0.0pt,depth=1.22917pt\picture(3.2,2.6)(-1.6,-1.0)\roundcap\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1,-1)(-1,-.5)(-1,0)\Line(-1,0)(-1,.5)\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1,-1)(1,-.5)(1,0)\Line(1,0)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1,.5)(-1,2)(1,2)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1.1,-1.1)(-.7,-1.9)(-.3,-1.5)\qbezier{}{-0.3}{-1.5}{0}{-1.2}{0.3}{-1.5}\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1.1,-1.1)(.7,-1.9)(.3,-1.5)\put(-0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\put(0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\endpicture}{\vrule width=0.0pt,height=0.0pt,depth=1.22917pt\picture(3.2,2.6)(-1.6,-1.0)\roundcap\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1,-1)(-1,-.5)(-1,0)\Line(-1,0)(-1,.5)\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1,-1)(1,-.5)(1,0)\Line(1,0)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1,.5)(-1,2)(1,2)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1.1,-1.1)(-.7,-1.9)(-.3,-1.5)\qbezier{}{-0.3}{-1.5}{0}{-1.2}{0.3}{-1.5}\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1.1,-1.1)(.7,-1.9)(.3,-1.5)\put(-0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\put(0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\endpicture}}},\omega,\gamma,\rho)
is

,
if the following holds:

(initial
\mathcal{O\!G}
)::

For every initial place

,
the following implication holds:

\left(\bigwedge_{v\in V_{\mathord{\mathchoice{\vrule width=0.0pt,height=0.0pt,depth=1.10419pt\picture(3.2,2.6)(-1.6,-1.0)\roundcap\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1,-1)(-1,-.5)(-1,0)\Line(-1,0)(-1,.5)\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1,-1)(1,-.5)(1,0)\Line(1,0)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1,.5)(-1,2)(1,2)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1.1,-1.1)(-.7,-1.9)(-.3,-1.5)\qbezier{}{-0.3}{-1.5}{0}{-1.2}{0.3}{-1.5}\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1.1,-1.1)(.7,-1.9)(.3,-1.5)\put(-0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\put(0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\endpicture}{\vrule width=0.0pt,height=0.0pt,depth=1.10419pt\picture(3.2,2.6)(-1.6,-1.0)\roundcap\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1,-1)(-1,-.5)(-1,0)\Line(-1,0)(-1,.5)\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1,-1)(1,-.5)(1,0)\Line(1,0)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1,.5)(-1,2)(1,2)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1.1,-1.1)(-.7,-1.9)(-.3,-1.5)\qbezier{}{-0.3}{-1.5}{0}{-1.2}{0.3}{-1.5}\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1.1,-1.1)(.7,-1.9)(.3,-1.5)\put(-0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\put(0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\endpicture}{\vrule width=0.0pt,height=0.0pt,depth=1.10419pt\picture(3.2,2.6)(-1.6,-1.0)\roundcap\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1,-1)(-1,-.5)(-1,0)\Line(-1,0)(-1,.5)\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1,-1)(1,-.5)(1,0)\Line(1,0)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1,.5)(-1,2)(1,2)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1.1,-1.1)(-.7,-1.9)(-.3,-1.5)\qbezier{}{-0.3}{-1.5}{0}{-1.2}{0.3}{-1.5}\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1.1,-1.1)(.7,-1.9)(.3,-1.5)\put(-0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\put(0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\endpicture}{\vrule width=0.0pt,height=0.0pt,depth=1.10419pt\picture(3.2,2.6)(-1.6,-1.0)\roundcap\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1,-1)(-1,-.5)(-1,0)\Line(-1,0)(-1,.5)\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1,-1)(1,-.5)(1,0)\Line(1,0)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1,.5)(-1,2)(1,2)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1.1,-1.1)(-.7,-1.9)(-.3,-1.5)\qbezier{}{-0.3}{-1.5}{0}{-1.2}{0.3}{-1.5}\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1.1,-1.1)(.7,-1.9)(.3,-1.5)\put(-0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\put(0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\endpicture}}}}v=\rho(v)\right)\models\omega(p)

(inductive
\mathcal{O\!G}
)::

For every transition
t
, the following Hoare triple holds:

{\left\{\bigwedge_{p\in{}^{\bullet}{t}}\omega(p)\middle\}\;\lambda(t)\,;\,\gamma(t)\;\middle\{\bigwedge_{p\in{t}^{\bullet}}\omega(p)\right\}}

(interference-free
\mathcal{O\!G}
)::

For every transition
t
, and every place
p
that is
co-marked
with
t
,
the following Hoare triple holds:

{\left\{\omega(p)\land\bigwedge_{p^{\prime}\in{}^{\bullet}{t}}\omega(p^{\prime})\middle\}\;\lambda(t)\,;\,\gamma(t)\;\middle\{\omega(p)\right\}}

(safe
\mathcal{O\!G}
)::

For every error place
p\in P_{\mathsf{err}}
, the annotation
\omega(p)
is equivalent to
\bot
.

Proposition
3.6
.

If there exists a valid Owicki-Gries annotation of
\mathcal{P}
,
then
\mathcal{P}
is correct.

Proof sketch.

Let
\mathcal{O\!G}=(V_{\mathord{\mathchoice{\vrule width=0.0pt,height=0.0pt,depth=1.22917pt\picture(3.2,2.6)(-1.6,-1.0)\roundcap\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1,-1)(-1,-.5)(-1,0)\Line(-1,0)(-1,.5)\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1,-1)(1,-.5)(1,0)\Line(1,0)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1,.5)(-1,2)(1,2)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1.1,-1.1)(-.7,-1.9)(-.3,-1.5)\qbezier{}{-0.3}{-1.5}{0}{-1.2}{0.3}{-1.5}\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1.1,-1.1)(.7,-1.9)(.3,-1.5)\put(-0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\put(0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\endpicture}{\vrule width=0.0pt,height=0.0pt,depth=1.22917pt\picture(3.2,2.6)(-1.6,-1.0)\roundcap\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1,-1)(-1,-.5)(-1,0)\Line(-1,0)(-1,.5)\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1,-1)(1,-.5)(1,0)\Line(1,0)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1,.5)(-1,2)(1,2)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1.1,-1.1)(-.7,-1.9)(-.3,-1.5)\qbezier{}{-0.3}{-1.5}{0}{-1.2}{0.3}{-1.5}\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1.1,-1.1)(.7,-1.9)(.3,-1.5)\put(-0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\put(0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\endpicture}{\vrule width=0.0pt,height=0.0pt,depth=1.22917pt\picture(3.2,2.6)(-1.6,-1.0)\roundcap\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1,-1)(-1,-.5)(-1,0)\Line(-1,0)(-1,.5)\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1,-1)(1,-.5)(1,0)\Line(1,0)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1,.5)(-1,2)(1,2)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1.1,-1.1)(-.7,-1.9)(-.3,-1.5)\qbezier{}{-0.3}{-1.5}{0}{-1.2}{0.3}{-1.5}\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1.1,-1.1)(.7,-1.9)(.3,-1.5)\put(-0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\put(0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\endpicture}{\vrule width=0.0pt,height=0.0pt,depth=1.22917pt\picture(3.2,2.6)(-1.6,-1.0)\roundcap\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1,-1)(-1,-.5)(-1,0)\Line(-1,0)(-1,.5)\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1,-1)(1,-.5)(1,0)\Line(1,0)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1,.5)(-1,2)(1,2)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1.1,-1.1)(-.7,-1.9)(-.3,-1.5)\qbezier{}{-0.3}{-1.5}{0}{-1.2}{0.3}{-1.5}\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1.1,-1.1)(.7,-1.9)(.3,-1.5)\put(-0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\put(0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\endpicture}}},\omega,\gamma,\rho)
be a valid Owicki-Gries annotation of
\mathcal{P}
.
Then the function
\beta:\textsc{Reach}\to\mathbf{Fm}(V_{\mathcal{P}})

with
\beta(m):=\exists V_{\mathord{\mathchoice{\vrule width=0.0pt,height=0.0pt,depth=1.22917pt\picture(3.2,2.6)(-1.6,-1.0)\roundcap\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1,-1)(-1,-.5)(-1,0)\Line(-1,0)(-1,.5)\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1,-1)(1,-.5)(1,0)\Line(1,0)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1,.5)(-1,2)(1,2)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1.1,-1.1)(-.7,-1.9)(-.3,-1.5)\qbezier{}{-0.3}{-1.5}{0}{-1.2}{0.3}{-1.5}\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1.1,-1.1)(.7,-1.9)(.3,-1.5)\put(-0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\put(0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\endpicture}{\vrule width=0.0pt,height=0.0pt,depth=1.22917pt\picture(3.2,2.6)(-1.6,-1.0)\roundcap\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1,-1)(-1,-.5)(-1,0)\Line(-1,0)(-1,.5)\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1,-1)(1,-.5)(1,0)\Line(1,0)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1,.5)(-1,2)(1,2)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1.1,-1.1)(-.7,-1.9)(-.3,-1.5)\qbezier{}{-0.3}{-1.5}{0}{-1.2}{0.3}{-1.5}\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1.1,-1.1)(.7,-1.9)(.3,-1.5)\put(-0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\put(0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\endpicture}{\vrule width=0.0pt,height=0.0pt,depth=1.22917pt\picture(3.2,2.6)(-1.6,-1.0)\roundcap\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1,-1)(-1,-.5)(-1,0)\Line(-1,0)(-1,.5)\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1,-1)(1,-.5)(1,0)\Line(1,0)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1,.5)(-1,2)(1,2)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1.1,-1.1)(-.7,-1.9)(-.3,-1.5)\qbezier{}{-0.3}{-1.5}{0}{-1.2}{0.3}{-1.5}\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1.1,-1.1)(.7,-1.9)(.3,-1.5)\put(-0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\put(0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\endpicture}{\vrule width=0.0pt,height=0.0pt,depth=1.22917pt\picture(3.2,2.6)(-1.6,-1.0)\roundcap\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1,-1)(-1,-.5)(-1,0)\Line(-1,0)(-1,.5)\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1,-1)(1,-.5)(1,0)\Line(1,0)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1,.5)(-1,2)(1,2)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1.1,-1.1)(-.7,-1.9)(-.3,-1.5)\qbezier{}{-0.3}{-1.5}{0}{-1.2}{0.3}{-1.5}\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1.1,-1.1)(.7,-1.9)(.3,-1.5)\put(-0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\put(0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\endpicture}}}\,.\,\bigwedge_{p\in m}\omega(p)
is a Floyd-Hoare annotation,
i.e.,
we have that
(i)

\beta(m_{\mathrm{init}})\equiv\top
,
that
(ii)

{\left\{\beta(m)\middle\}\;\lambda(t)\;\middle\{\beta(m^{\prime})\right\}}
whenever
m\mathrel{\triangleright_{t}}m^{\prime}
,
and that
(iii)

\beta(m)\equiv\bot
if
m\cap P_{\mathsf{err}}\neq\emptyset
.
By sequential composition of Hoare triples,
the Hoare triple
{\left\{\top\middle\}\;\lambda(t_{1})\ldots\lambda(t_{n})\;\middle\{\bot\right\}}
holds
for every firing sequence
m_{\mathrm{init}}=m_{0}\mathrel{\triangleright_{t_{1}}}\ldots\mathrel{\triangleright_{t_{n}}}=m_{n}

where
m_{n}\cap P_{\mathsf{err}}\neq\emptyset
,
and
\mathcal{P}
is correct.
∎

) and updated when
x
is incremented.
The annotation
\omega(p)
for each place
p
includes a case distinction on the ghost variable of the other thread to reflect whether it has already incremented
x
.
For example, in
p_{5}
,
x>1
holds if the left-hand thread has already executed its increment, and
x>0
otherwise.

As the example shows, Owicki-Gries annotations can provide a concise correctness argument for Petri programs,
as opposed to, say, specifying an inductive invariant for each of the 17 reachable markings of the example program.
Owicki-Gries annotations can be
validated
efficiently: given the components
(V_{\mathord{\mathchoice{\vrule width=0.0pt,height=0.0pt,depth=1.22917pt\picture(3.2,2.6)(-1.6,-1.0)\roundcap\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1,-1)(-1,-.5)(-1,0)\Line(-1,0)(-1,.5)\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1,-1)(1,-.5)(1,0)\Line(1,0)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1,.5)(-1,2)(1,2)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1.1,-1.1)(-.7,-1.9)(-.3,-1.5)\qbezier{}{-0.3}{-1.5}{0}{-1.2}{0.3}{-1.5}\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1.1,-1.1)(.7,-1.9)(.3,-1.5)\put(-0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\put(0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\endpicture}{\vrule width=0.0pt,height=0.0pt,depth=1.22917pt\picture(3.2,2.6)(-1.6,-1.0)\roundcap\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1,-1)(-1,-.5)(-1,0)\Line(-1,0)(-1,.5)\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1,-1)(1,-.5)(1,0)\Line(1,0)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1,.5)(-1,2)(1,2)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1.1,-1.1)(-.7,-1.9)(-.3,-1.5)\qbezier{}{-0.3}{-1.5}{0}{-1.2}{0.3}{-1.5}\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1.1,-1.1)(.7,-1.9)(.3,-1.5)\put(-0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\put(0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\endpicture}{\vrule width=0.0pt,height=0.0pt,depth=1.22917pt\picture(3.2,2.6)(-1.6,-1.0)\roundcap\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1,-1)(-1,-.5)(-1,0)\Line(-1,0)(-1,.5)\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1,-1)(1,-.5)(1,0)\Line(1,0)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1,.5)(-1,2)(1,2)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1.1,-1.1)(-.7,-1.9)(-.3,-1.5)\qbezier{}{-0.3}{-1.5}{0}{-1.2}{0.3}{-1.5}\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1.1,-1.1)(.7,-1.9)(.3,-1.5)\put(-0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\put(0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\endpicture}{\vrule width=0.0pt,height=0.0pt,depth=1.22917pt\picture(3.2,2.6)(-1.6,-1.0)\roundcap\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1,-1)(-1,-.5)(-1,0)\Line(-1,0)(-1,.5)\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1,-1)(1,-.5)(1,0)\Line(1,0)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1,.5)(-1,2)(1,2)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1.1,-1.1)(-.7,-1.9)(-.3,-1.5)\qbezier{}{-0.3}{-1.5}{0}{-1.2}{0.3}{-1.5}\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1.1,-1.1)(.7,-1.9)(.3,-1.5)\put(-0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\put(0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\endpicture}}},\omega,\gamma,\rho)
,
it requires only quadratically many (in the size of the program) satisfiability and Hoare triple checks to determine whether the four validity conditions of
Definition

3.5
hold.
Special attention has to be paid to
(interference-free
\mathcal{O\!G}
)
,
as it references the
co-marked
relation between places and transitions.
Computing this relation from scratch for arbitrary Petri programs would lead to exponential validation cost,
though unfolding techniques
(
McMillan 1992
)
can reduce the practical impact.
However, for Petri programs derived from other representations such as program code
(
Heizmann et al. 2024
)
,
the co-marked relation can be constructed efficiently along with the Petri program itself.

It is well-known that Owicki-Gries proofs of correct programs can always be constructed
by introducing ghost variables that track the control locations of all threads,
as shown by
Lamport 1977

(
Lamport 1977
)
.
A similar result applies in our formalism.
We refer to the resulting annotations as the “naïve” Owicki-Gries annotations, to distinguish them from the method developed in the rest of the paper.
As Petri programs do not have a clear notion of threads, we encode the control state by introducing boolean ghost variables for each place, indicating whether the program is currently at that place.
This yields a linear overhead in the number of places compared to
Lamport 1977
’s approach.

Definition 3.8.

Let
(A,\mathit{post})
be a safe invariant domain for
\mathcal{P}
.

and
\beta(m)\coloneq\bigvee_{\langle m,\varphi\rangle\in\textsc{ReachConf}(A,\mathit{post})}\varphi

In the above definition, the function
\beta
is an inductive annotation (or
Floyd-Hoare annotation
) of the reachability graph formed by all reachable markings.
The formula
\tilde{\theta}(m)
constrains the ghost variables to express that the current marking of
\mathcal{P}
is exactly the marking
m
.

3.9
.

\mathcal{O\!G}_{\textrm{na\"{i}ve}}(A,\mathit{post})
is valid.

Example 3.10.

Fig.

2
),
and the invariant domain
(A,\mathit{post})

\mathit{post}\left(\varphi,t\right)
is the strongest formula in

.

Figure

4
shows an excerpt of the naïve Owicki-Gries annotation
\mathcal{O\!G}_{\textrm{na\"{i}ve}}(A,\mathit{post})
.

This construction provides a canonical way to construct Owicki-Gries annotations from safe invariant domains.
However, the resulting annotations are, of course, deeply unsatisfying.
The annotation
\omega(p)
of each place
p
consists of a large disjunction over all reachable markings containing
p
, encoding the full reachability graph of the Petri program.
The size of these formulas is typically exponential in the size of
\mathcal{P}
.
Hence, we cannot hope to efficiently validate such Owicki-Gries annotations.
Our evaluation in
Section

7
shows that the explosion in size and validation time is not purely theoretic, but leads to prohibitively bad performance on practical benchmarks.

4.
Empires: An Abstract Representation of Interleaving Information

The key challenge of constructing compact Owicki-Gries annotations lies in the synthesis of ghost variables that contain enough interleaving information, without being too fine-grained.
As the naïve encoding of the entire global control state leads to unreasonably large Owicki-Gries annotations,
a coarser abstraction of interleaving information is needed.
To this end, we present the notion of
empires
, as an intermediate step on the way from invariant domains to Owicki-Gries annotations.
Empires capture sufficient interleaving information to allow for the construction of valid Owicki-Gries annotations,
while allowing to abstract away irrelevant details of the interleaving, and specifically to summarize multiple global control states if they need not be distinguished.
We begin by explaining the basic concepts underlying empires,
and subsequently show how to construct an Owicki-Gries annotation by using ghost variables to encode an empire.

The first step towards empires consists in defining a suitable abstraction of the given program’s global control state.
That is, instead of distinguishing every reachable marking,
we need an abstract representation for a set of markings.
To this end, observe that in the naïve Owicki-Gries annotation of
Fig.

4
,
the ghost variables record precisely for all places whether they have a token or not.
However, we have seen in
Fig.

3
,
that it suffices to record for each thread, whether it has already incremented
x
.
We do not need to distinguish whether the right-hand thread is currently in
p_{5}
or in
p_{6}
.
Hence, we group places such as this in a
region
.

Definition 4.1.

A
region

r
is a non-empty set of places (
\emptyset\subset r\subseteq P
)
such that for all
p_{1},p_{2}\in r
,
it holds that
p_{1}
and
p_{2}
are not co-related.
The set of all regions is denoted by
\mathbf{Regions}
.

A region can be thought of as an abstraction of the
local
control state of a thread.
As the notion of threads does not exist in Petri programs, we here capture this intuition by the condition that the places must not be co-related.
Typically (and always in the construction presented in
Section

5
), regions are segments of places that are connected by sequential transitions, i.e., transitions with only a single predecessor and successor place (no fork or join transitions).
Branching inside a region is also possible, i.e., a place in the region may have multiple outgoing (or incoming) sequential transitions to (resp. from) other places in the region.

An abstraction of the local control state gives rise to a corresponding abstraction of the global control state, i.e., of the Petri program’s markings.
Intuitively, we take one region to represent the local control state of each thread.
Formally, this idea is captured by
territories
.

Definition 4.2.

A
territory

\tau
is a set of pairwise disjoint regions
\{r_{1},\dots,r_{n}\}
,
such that every set
m
derived by taking exactly one place from each region in
\tau
is a reachable marking.
The set of all such markings is denoted by
\mathit{treaty}(\tau)
.
We write
\mathbf{Terr}
for the set of all territories.

A territory
\tau
is an abstract representation of the markings in
\mathit{treaty}(\tau)
.
To update this representation when the program takes a step, we extend the firing relation from markings to territories.

Definition 4.3.

A transition
t
is
enabled
in a territory
\tau
, denoted by
t\in\mathit{enabled}(\tau)
,
if for every predecessor place
p\in{}^{\bullet}{t}
,
there exists a distinct region
r_{p}\in\tau
with
p\in r_{p}

(i.e.,
r_{p_{1}}\neq r_{p_{2}}
if
p_{1}\neq p_{2}
).
We write
\tau\mathrel{\triangleright_{t}}\tau^{\prime}
for territory
\tau^{\prime}

if

\tau^{\prime}=\big(\tau\setminus\{\,r_{p}\mid p\in{}^{\bullet}{t}\,\}\big)\cup\{\,r^{\prime}_{p^{\prime}}\mid p^{\prime}\in{t}^{\bullet}\,\}\ ,

where for each successor place
p^{\prime}\in{t}^{\bullet}
,
r^{\prime}_{p^{\prime}}
is a distinct region containing
p^{\prime}

(i.e.,
r^{\prime}_{p_{1}^{\prime}}\neq r^{\prime}_{p_{2}^{\prime}}
if
p_{1}^{\prime}\neq p_{2}^{\prime}
).
The regions in the set
\tau\setminus\{\,r_{p}\mid p\in{}^{\bullet}{t}\,\}
are called
bystanders
,
and can be equivalently written as

\mathit{bystanders}_{t}(\tau)=\{\,r\in\tau\mid r\cap{}^{\bullet}{t}=\emptyset\,\}\ .

The notion of enabled transitions and the firing relation
\mathrel{\triangleright_{t}}
on territories naturally reflect the corresponding notions on the markings represented by the territories.

Proposition
4.4
.

Transition
t
is enabled in a territory
\tau
iff there exists
m\in\mathit{treaty}(\tau)
that enables
t
.
If
\tau\mathrel{\triangleright_{t}}\tau^{\prime}
holds,
then for all
m\in\mathit{treaty}(\tau)
and
m^{\prime}
with
m\mathrel{\triangleright_{t}}m^{\prime}
, it follows that
m^{\prime}\in\mathit{treaty}(\tau^{\prime})
.

Note that, while the firing relation on markings is deterministic (for every
m
and
t
, there is at most one marking
m^{\prime}
with
m\mathrel{\triangleright_{t}}m^{\prime}
),
the firing relation on territories is nondeterministic.
There can be multiple territories
\tau^{\prime}
such that
\tau\mathrel{\triangleright_{t}}\tau^{\prime}
, each representing a different set of markings.

Proposition

4.4
prescribes only a minimal set of markings that must be represented by
\tau^{\prime}
.
The choice of a “good” territory
\tau^{\prime}
is a key step in our empire construction presented in
Section

5
.

Having fixed our abstraction of the global control state,
we are ready to introduce empires.
As explained above, the purpose of empires is to capture interleaving information that is relevant to prove correctness.
Hence, we define an empire as a
state machine
,
which can update its state whenever a new transition is executed and appended to the current interleaving.
Each state of the empire is labeled by a territory, describing the current global control state,
and a formula over the program variables (the “law” of the state),
describing the current values of the program variables.

Definition 4.5 (Empire).

An
empire
is a finite state machine
E=(Q,q_{\mathrm{init}},\delta,\mathit{law},\mathit{terr})

consisting of
a finite set of states
Q
,
an initial state
q_{\mathrm{init}}\in Q
,
a (partial) transition function
\delta:Q\times T\rightharpoonup Q
,
a law mapping
\mathit{law}:Q\rightarrow\mathbf{Fm}(V_{\mathcal{P}})
labeling each state with a formula, and
a territory mapping
\mathit{terr}:Q\rightarrow\textbf{Terr}
labeling each state with a territory.

Example 4.6.

An example of an empire is shown in
Fig.

5
.
Here, the states use territories to group multiple markings in the same state (e.g., the territory for
q_{2}
abstracts the four markings
\{p_{2},p_{5}\},\{p_{2},p_{6}\},\{p_{3},p_{5}\},\{p_{3},p_{6}\}
).
As a result, the annotation remains compact, since it avoids separate states for different markings with the same law.

Definition 4.7.

An empire
E=(Q,q_{\mathrm{init}},\delta,\mathit{law},\mathit{terr})
is
valid

if the following conditions hold:

(initial-law
E
)::

\mathit{law}(q_{\mathrm{init}})\equiv\top

(initial-territory
E
)::

m_{\mathrm{init}}\in\mathit{treaty}(\mathit{terr}(q_{\mathrm{init}}))

(inductive-law
E
)::

For all
q\in Q,t\in\mathit{enabled}(\mathit{terr}(q))
, one of the following holds:

•:

q^{\prime}\coloneq\delta(q,t)
is defined and the Hoare triple

{\left\{\mathit{law}(q)\middle\}\;\lambda(t)\;\middle\{\mathit{law}(q^{\prime})\right\}}
holds, or

•:

the Hoare triple
{\left\{\mathit{law}(q)\middle\}\;\lambda(t)\;\middle\{\bot\right\}}
holds.

(inductive-territory
E
)::

For all
q,q^{\prime}\in Q,t\in T
with
\delta(q,t)=q^{\prime}
,
\mathit{terr}(q)\mathrel{\triangleright_{t}}\mathit{terr}(q^{\prime})
holds.

(safe
E
)::

For all states
q\in Q
such that there exists a region
r\in\mathit{terr}(q)
with
r\cap P_{\mathsf{err}}\neq\emptyset
, it holds that
\mathit{law}(q)\equiv\bot
.

The first four validity conditions ensure that the law and territory of the state reached after some interleaving
soundly reflect the values of the program variables resp. the control state at that point.
The second alternative of
(inductive-law
E
)
optionally allows omitting edges
if the law is sufficient to prove that executing some transition is infeasible.
The last condition
(safe
E
)
ensures that the empire has sufficient information to prove correctness of the program.
Note that, in particular,
(safe
E
)
may hold vacuously,
as all edges to states with law
\bot
can be omitted.

Theorem
4.8
.

If there exists a valid empire for the Petri program
\mathcal{P}
, then
\mathcal{P}
is correct.

Proof sketch.

Let
E=(Q,q_{\mathrm{init}},\delta,\mathit{law},\mathit{terr})
be a valid empire for
\mathcal{P}
.
Assume wlog. that for all
q,q^{\prime}\in Q
with
q\neq q^{\prime}
,
the formulae
\mathit{law}(q)
and
\mathit{law}(q^{\prime})
differ (at least syntactically).
Then
(A,\mathit{post})
is a safe invariant domain,
with
A=\{\,\mathit{law}(q)\mid q\in Q\,\}\cup\{\bot\}
, where
\mathit{post}\left(\bot,t\right)=\bot
and

\mathit{post}\left(\mathit{law}(q),t\right)=\begin{cases}\mathit{law}(\delta(q,t))&\textbf{if }\delta(q,t)\text{ is defined}\\
\top&\textbf{if }t\text{ is not enabled in }\mathit{terr}(q)\\
\bot&\textbf{otherwise}\end{cases}\vskip-15.00002pt

∎

q_{0}

\{\{p_{0}\}\}
\top
q_{1}

\{\{p_{1}\},\{p_{5},p_{6}\}\}
x>0
q_{2}

\{\{p_{2},p_{3}\},\{p_{5},p_{6}\}\}
x>0\land y\leq x
q_{3}

\{\{p_{1}\},\{p_{7}\}\}
x>1
q_{4}

\{\{p_{4}\},\{p_{5},p_{6}\}\}
x>1\land y<x
q_{5}

\{\{p_{2},p_{3}\},\{p_{7}\}\}
x>1\land y\leq x
q_{6}

\{\{p_{2},p_{3}\},\{p_{7}\}\}
x>1\land y\leq x
q_{7}

\{\{p_{4}\},\{p_{7}\}\}
x>2\land y<x

x>0

y:=x

x:=x+1

x:=x+1

x:=x+1

y:=x

x:=x+1

x:=x+1

x:=x+1

\hbox to22.71pt{\vbox to11.19pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip-2.0pt\lower-2.84164pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} {{}{}{{

Figure 5
.
A valid empire for the Petri program in
Fig.

2
.

As empires are intended to be an intermediate step on the way from invariant domains to Owicki-Gries annotations,
we have to show (1) how to derive an empire from an invariant domain
(A,\mathit{post})
, and (2) how to construct an Owicki-Gries annotation from an empire
E=(Q,q_{\mathrm{init}},\delta,\mathit{law},\mathit{terr})
.
We present our construction for problem (1) in
Section

5
below.
As for problem (2), the key idea is to introduce a single ghost variable, named
\mathord{\mathchoice{\vrule width=0.0pt,height=0.0pt,depth=1.50002pt\picture(3.2,2.6)(-1.6,-1.0)\roundcap\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1,-1)(-1,-.5)(-1,0)\Line(-1,0)(-1,.5)\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1,-1)(1,-.5)(1,0)\Line(1,0)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1,.5)(-1,2)(1,2)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1.1,-1.1)(-.7,-1.9)(-.3,-1.5)\qbezier{}{-0.3}{-1.5}{0}{-1.2}{0.3}{-1.5}\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1.1,-1.1)(.7,-1.9)(.3,-1.5)\put(-0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\put(0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\endpicture}{\vrule width=0.0pt,height=0.0pt,depth=1.50002pt\picture(3.2,2.6)(-1.6,-1.0)\roundcap\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1,-1)(-1,-.5)(-1,0)\Line(-1,0)(-1,.5)\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1,-1)(1,-.5)(1,0)\Line(1,0)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1,.5)(-1,2)(1,2)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1.1,-1.1)(-.7,-1.9)(-.3,-1.5)\qbezier{}{-0.3}{-1.5}{0}{-1.2}{0.3}{-1.5}\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1.1,-1.1)(.7,-1.9)(.3,-1.5)\put(-0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\put(0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\endpicture}{\vrule width=0.0pt,height=0.0pt,depth=1.50002pt\picture(3.2,2.6)(-1.6,-1.0)\roundcap\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1,-1)(-1,-.5)(-1,0)\Line(-1,0)(-1,.5)\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1,-1)(1,-.5)(1,0)\Line(1,0)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1,.5)(-1,2)(1,2)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1.1,-1.1)(-.7,-1.9)(-.3,-1.5)\qbezier{}{-0.3}{-1.5}{0}{-1.2}{0.3}{-1.5}\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1.1,-1.1)(.7,-1.9)(.3,-1.5)\put(-0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\put(0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\endpicture}{\vrule width=0.0pt,height=0.0pt,depth=1.50002pt\picture(3.2,2.6)(-1.6,-1.0)\roundcap\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1,-1)(-1,-.5)(-1,0)\Line(-1,0)(-1,.5)\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1,-1)(1,-.5)(1,0)\Line(1,0)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1,.5)(-1,2)(1,2)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1.1,-1.1)(-.7,-1.9)(-.3,-1.5)\qbezier{}{-0.3}{-1.5}{0}{-1.2}{0.3}{-1.5}\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1.1,-1.1)(.7,-1.9)(.3,-1.5)\put(-0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\put(0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\endpicture}}
, that tracks the current state of the empire.
We use the empire’s territories to determine the possible empire states whenever a token is in a given place
p
,
namely the states
Q_{p}\coloneq\{\,q\in Q\mid\exists r\in\mathit{terr}(q)\,.\,p\in r\,\}
.
By taking the laws of these possible states, we constrain the possible program states when there is a token in
p
.

Definition 4.9.

Let
E=(Q,q_{\mathrm{init}},\delta,\mathit{law},\mathit{terr})
be an empire.
The
imperial Owicki-Gries annotation

that ranges over the states of the empire.

(3)

There is an additional ghost update for the transition

y:=x

.

We address difference (1) in
Section

6
.
(2) is mainly a matter of preference, since the ghost variable encodes the same information. We plan to address the slight overhead introduced by the additional ghost update (3) in future work.

It remains to show that if the empire
E
is valid, then
\mathcal{O\!G}_{E}
is a valid Owicki-Gries annotation.
The following lemma presents a first key observation towards this result.

Lemma
4.11
.

Let
\hat{P}
be a nonempty subset of a reachable marking,
and let us write
Q_{\hat{P}}
for the intersection
\bigcap_{p\in\hat{P}}Q_{p}
.
Then it holds that

\bigwedge_{p\in\hat{P}}\omega(p)\equiv\bigvee_{q\in Q_{\hat{P}}}\big(\mathord{\mathchoice{\vrule width=0.0pt,height=0.0pt,depth=1.50002pt\picture(3.2,2.6)(-1.6,-1.0)\roundcap\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1,-1)(-1,-.5)(-1,0)\Line(-1,0)(-1,.5)\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1,-1)(1,-.5)(1,0)\Line(1,0)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1,.5)(-1,2)(1,2)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1.1,-1.1)(-.7,-1.9)(-.3,-1.5)\qbezier{}{-0.3}{-1.5}{0}{-1.2}{0.3}{-1.5}\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1.1,-1.1)(.7,-1.9)(.3,-1.5)\put(-0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\put(0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\endpicture}{\vrule width=0.0pt,height=0.0pt,depth=1.50002pt\picture(3.2,2.6)(-1.6,-1.0)\roundcap\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1,-1)(-1,-.5)(-1,0)\Line(-1,0)(-1,.5)\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1,-1)(1,-.5)(1,0)\Line(1,0)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1,.5)(-1,2)(1,2)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1.1,-1.1)(-.7,-1.9)(-.3,-1.5)\qbezier{}{-0.3}{-1.5}{0}{-1.2}{0.3}{-1.5}\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1.1,-1.1)(.7,-1.9)(.3,-1.5)\put(-0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\put(0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\endpicture}{\vrule width=0.0pt,height=0.0pt,depth=1.50002pt\picture(3.2,2.6)(-1.6,-1.0)\roundcap\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1,-1)(-1,-.5)(-1,0)\Line(-1,0)(-1,.5)\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1,-1)(1,-.5)(1,0)\Line(1,0)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1,.5)(-1,2)(1,2)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1.1,-1.1)(-.7,-1.9)(-.3,-1.5)\qbezier{}{-0.3}{-1.5}{0}{-1.2}{0.3}{-1.5}\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1.1,-1.1)(.7,-1.9)(.3,-1.5)\put(-0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\put(0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\endpicture}{\vrule width=0.0pt,height=0.0pt,depth=1.50002pt\picture(3.2,2.6)(-1.6,-1.0)\roundcap\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1,-1)(-1,-.5)(-1,0)\Line(-1,0)(-1,.5)\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1,-1)(1,-.5)(1,0)\Line(1,0)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1,.5)(-1,2)(1,2)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1.1,-1.1)(-.7,-1.9)(-.3,-1.5)\qbezier{}{-0.3}{-1.5}{0}{-1.2}{0.3}{-1.5}\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1.1,-1.1)(.7,-1.9)(.3,-1.5)\put(-0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\put(0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\endpicture}}=q\land\mathit{law}(q)\big)\ .

Informally, the lemma shows that we can combine the annotations of multiple places in a marking,
and recover the same information as given by directly computing the possible empire states for the set of places
\hat{P}
.
This is crucial to show the
(interference-free
\mathcal{O\!G}
)
property of Owicki-Gries annotations,
where we consider
\hat{P}={}^{\bullet}{t}\cup\{p\}
for a place
p
that is co-marked with
t
,
as well as for the
(inductive
\mathcal{O\!G}
)
property of transitions
t
with multiple predecessor places (“joins”), with
\hat{P}={}^{\bullet}{t}
.

Lemma

4.11
allows us to break down these properties to individual empire states,
and we observe:

Lemma 4.12.

Let
q,q^{\prime}\in Q
and
t
a transition with
\delta(q,t)=q^{\prime}
.
The following Hoare triple holds:

{\left\{\mathord{\mathchoice{\vrule width=0.0pt,height=0.0pt,depth=1.50002pt\picture(3.2,2.6)(-1.6,-1.0)\roundcap\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1,-1)(-1,-.5)(-1,0)\Line(-1,0)(-1,.5)\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1,-1)(1,-.5)(1,0)\Line(1,0)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1,.5)(-1,2)(1,2)(1,.5)\cbezier(-1.5,-1.5)(-1.1,-1.1)(-.7,-1.9)(-.3,-1.5)\qbezier{}{-0.3}{-1.5}{0}{-1.2}{0.3}{-1.5}\cbezier(1.5,-1.5)(1.1,-1.1)(.7,-1.9)(.3,-1.5)\put(-0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\put(0.4,0.7){\circle*{0.4}}\endpicture}{\vrule Proof.

This follows from
(inductive-law
E
)
and the definition of
\gamma
.
∎

Putting these observations together,
we show:

Lemma

.

Let

be a nonempty subset of a reachable marking,

{\left\{\bigwedge_{p\in\hat{P}}\omega(p)\middle\}\;\lambda(t);\gamma(t)\;\middle\{\bigwedge_{p\in(\hat{P}\setminus{}^{\bullet}{t})\cup{t}^{\bullet}}\omega(p)\right\}}

.

(safe
\mathcal{O\!G}
)
::

Let
p\in P_{\mathsf{err}}
be an error place.
By
(safe
E
)
, it holds for every
q\in Q_{p}

that
\mathit{law}(q)\equiv\bot
.
Hence, we have
\omega(p)\equiv\bot
.

(inductive
\mathcal{O\!G}
)
::

Let
t
be a transition.
From
Lemma

4.13
,
using
\hat{P}={}^{\bullet}{t}
,
it follows that the Hoare triple
{\left\{\bigwedge_{p\in{}^{\bullet}{t}}\omega(p)\middle\}\;\lambda(t);\gamma(t)\;\middle\{\bigwedge_{p\in({}^{\bullet}{t}\setminus{}^{\bullet}{t})\cup{t}^{\bullet}}\omega(p)\right\}}
holds.
By simplifying the postcondition, we observe that the Hoare triple
{\left\{\bigwedge_{p\in{}^{\bullet}{t}}\omega(p)\middle\}\;\lambda(t);\gamma(t)\;\middle\{\bigwedge_{p\in{t}^{\bullet}}\omega(p)\right\}}
holds.

(interference-free
\mathcal{O\!G}
)
::

Let
t
be a transition and
p
a place that is co-marked with
t
.
It follows that
the Hoare triple
{\left\{\bigwedge_{p^{\prime}\in{}^{\bullet}{t}\cup\{p\}}\omega(p^{\prime})\middle\}\;\lambda(t);\gamma(t)\;\middle\{\bigwedge_{p^{\prime}\in({}^{\bullet}{t}\cup\{p\}\setminus{}^{\bullet}{t})\cup{t}^{\bullet}}\omega(p^{\prime})\right\}}
must hold,
by applying
Lemma

4.13
for
\hat{P}={}^{\bullet}{t}\cup\{p\}
.
By weakening the postcondition, we conclude that the Hoare triple
{\left\{\omega(p)\land\bigwedge_{p^{\prime}\in{}^{\bullet}{t}}\omega(p^{\prime})\middle\}\;\lambda(t);\gamma(t)\;\middle\{\omega(p)\right\}}
also holds.

As all four validity conditions must hold,
\mathcal{O\!G}_{E}
is valid.
∎

5.
Constructing Empires by Symbolic Execution

As
Section

4
shows how Owicki-Gries annotations can be derived from empires,
the remaining central step of our method is to construct an empire for a Petri program from a given safe invariant domain.
In this section, we present two algorithms that construct such empires step-by-step, through a symbolic execution of the program.
The first algorithm is straightforward, but produces unnecessarily large empires.
We then show how this algorithm can be improved by grouping more places into regions, resulting in the second algorithm, the construction of
saturated empires
.

5.1.
A Straightforward Approach to Constructing Empires

In the following, let
(A,\mathit{post})
be a safe invariant domain for the Petri program
\mathcal{P}
.
We begin our construction by defining the type of states in the empire.
By the definition of empires, each state
q
of an empire must be labeled with a territory
\mathit{terr}(q)
and a formula
\mathit{law}(q)
.
Hence, the most straightforward representation of an empire state is a pair
q=\langle\tau,\varphi\rangle
of a territory
\tau
and a formula
\varphi
,
allowing us to set
\mathit{terr}(q)=\tau
and
\mathit{law}(q)=\varphi
.
Specifically, we always use a formula
\varphi\in A
given by the invariant domain.
We proceed by considering each of the four validity conditions for empires (
Definition

4.7
) in turn,
each time choosing the simplest way to satisfy it.

Let us begin by considering the initial state
q_{\mathrm{init}}
.
Per
(initial-territory
E
)
, the territory
\mathit{terr}(q_{\mathrm{init}})
must represent at least the initial marking
m_{\mathrm{init}}
.
Hence, the simplest choice is the territory which represents exactly
m_{\mathrm{init}}
and no other markings.
Each place in
m_{\mathrm{init}}
forms a singleton region.

\tau_{\mathrm{init}}\coloneq\big\{\,\{p\}\mid p\in m_{\mathrm{init}}\,\big\}

Furthermore, as
(initial-law
E
)
fixes the formula
\mathit{law}(q_{\mathrm{init}})
to be
\top
,
we must define
q_{\mathrm{init}}\coloneq\langle\tau_{\mathrm{init}},\top\rangle
.

The remaining states of the empire are constructed step-by-step, through symbolic execution.
Recursively, for every state
\langle\tau,\varphi\rangle
, and every transition
t
that is enabled in
\tau
,
a successor state
\delta(\langle\tau,\varphi\rangle,t)=\langle\tau^{\prime},\varphi^{\prime}\rangle
is computed.
To determine a suitable successor state, let us first examine the territory
\tau^{\prime}
.
The condition
(inductive-territory
E
)
prescribes that
\tau\mathrel{\triangleright_{t}}\tau^{\prime}
must hold.
This means that
\tau^{\prime}
must contain a (distinct) region for each successor place of
t
,
and all bystander regions of
\tau
(i.e., regions that are not involved in the transition, in the sense that they do not contain a predecessor place of
t
) must be preserved.
Hence, the simplest choice for
\tau^{\prime}
is as follows:

Definition 5.1.

Let
\tau
be a territory, and
t\in\mathit{enabled}(\tau)
a transition.
The
replaced territory
is

\mathit{replaced}_{t}(\tau)\coloneq\mathit{bystanders}_{t}(\tau)\cup\big\{\,\{p\}\mid p\in t^{\bullet}\,\big\}\ .

Lemma
5.2
.

\mathit{replaced}_{t}(\tau)
is a territory, and it holds that
\tau\mathrel{\triangleright_{t}}\mathit{replaced}_{t}(\tau)
.

It remains only to choose a suitable successor law
\varphi^{\prime}
.
Condition
(inductive-law
E
)
requires that the Hoare triple
{\left\{\varphi\middle\}\;\lambda(t)\;\middle\{\varphi^{\prime}\right\}}
must hold.
By the definition of invariant domains, this is always satisfied if we choose
\varphi^{\prime}:=\mathit{post}\left(\varphi,t\right)
.
Moreover,
(inductive-law
E
)
allows us to omit the edge from the empire if
{\left\{\varphi\middle\}\;\lambda(t)\;\middle\{\bot\right\}}
holds.
Hence, we let
\delta(\langle\tau,\varphi\rangle,t)
be undefined whenever
\mathit{post}\left(\varphi,t\right)=\bot
.
The following definition summarizes our discussion:

Definition
5.3
.

The
naïve empire

E_{\textrm{na\"{i}ve}}(A,\mathit{post})
corresponding to an invariant domain
(A,\mathit{post})

is the reachable part of the empire
(Q,q_{\mathrm{init}},\delta,\mathit{law},\mathit{terr})
,
where

•

the states
Q\coloneq\mathbf{Terr}\times A
are pairs of territories and laws;

•

the initial state is
q_{\mathrm{init}}\coloneq\langle\tau_{\mathrm{init}},\top\rangle
,
with the territory
\tau_{\mathrm{init}}=\big\{\,\{p\}\mid p\in m_{\mathrm{init}}\,\big\}
;

•

the partial transition function
\delta
is defined as

\delta(\langle\tau,\varphi\rangle,t)\coloneq\begin{cases}\text{undefined}&\textbf{if }t\not\in\mathit{enabled}(\tau)\text{ or }\mathit{post}\left(\varphi,t\right)\equiv\bot\\
\langle\mathit{replaced}_{t}(\tau),\mathit{post}\left(\varphi,t\right)\rangle&\textbf{otherwise}\end{cases}

•

and the law and territory mappings are given by
\mathit{law}(\langle\tau,\varphi\rangle)\coloneq\varphi
resp.
\mathit{terr}(\langle\tau,\varphi\rangle)\coloneq\tau
.

The restriction to the reachable part is required to ensure the condition
(safe
E
)
. We show:

Proposition
5.4
.

If the invariant domain
(A,\mathit{post})
is safe,
the naïve empire
E_{\textrm{na\"{i}ve}}(A,\mathit{post})
is valid.

Hence, we can construct a valid Owicki-Gries construction from the naïve empire.
However, as one may expect when always choosing the simplest and most straightforward solution,
the results are far from satisfactory:
The naïve construction does not utilize regions and territories in their full capacity.
Each region in the empire consists of a single place, and every territory thus represents only a single marking.
Hence, the imperial Owicki-Gries annotation for the naïve empire resembles the naïve Owicki-Gries annotation (
Definition

3.8
):
Each transition is annotated with a ghost update, and
the annotation
\omega(p)
of each place
p
is a case distinction over all reachable markings containing
p
.
The size of the annotation is typically exponential in the size of the program.

q_{0}

\{\{p_{0}\}\}
\top
q_{1}^{1}

\{\{p_{1}\},\{p_{5}\}\}
x>0
q_{1}^{2}

\{\{p_{1}\},\{p_{6}\}\}
x>0
q_{2}^{1}

\{\{p_{2}\},\{p_{5}\}\}
x>0\land y\leq x
q_{3}

\{\{p_{1}\},\{p_{7}\}\}
x>1
q_{2}^{2}

\{\{p_{2}\},\{p_{6}\}\}
x>0\land y\leq x
\ldots
\ldots

y:=x

z:=z/x

Figure 7
.
Excerpt of the naïve empire for the Petri program in
Fig.

2
.

For the example program from
Fig.

2
, the excerpt of the naïve empire in
Fig.

7
demonstrates the disadvantages of this construction.
For each reachable marking, there exists at least one state in the empire.
As shown in
Fig.

5
, territories can represent multiple markings.
For example, state
q_{1}
contains the region
\{p_{5},p_{6}\}
in its territory.
The naïve empire requires two states
q_{1}^{1}
and
q_{1}^{2}
to represent those markings.
For
q_{2}
, the naïve approach even requires four different states.

5.2.
Constructing Larger Regions

To derive compact Owicki-Gries annotations,
we must construct empires with fewer states,
grouping multiple consecutive places of a thread into one region.
To this end, we introduce a condition that allows us to
extend
an existing territory by executing one of its enabled transitions.
The result is a larger territory (representing a superset of markings).
We consider only
sequential transitions
, with a single predecessor place and a single successor place,
as forking or joining a thread requires creating a new territory (all markings represented by a territory have the same number of tokens).

Definition 5.5.

Let
\tau
be a territory, and let
t
be a transition.
We say that
\tau
is
extendable with
t

if
t
is enabled in
\tau
,
we have
{}^{\bullet}{t}=\{p\}
,
{t}^{\bullet}=\{p^{\prime}\}
for places
p,p^{\prime}
,
and for all places
p^{\prime\prime}
in the region
r\in\tau
with
p\in r
, it holds that
p^{\prime}
and
p^{\prime\prime}
are not co-related.
The
extended territory
is defined as

\mathit{extended}_{t}(\tau)\coloneq\mathit{bystanders}_{t}(\tau)\cup\big\{\,r\cup\{p^{\prime}\}\,\big\}\ .

Lemma
5.6
.

If
\tau
is extendable with
t
, then
\mathit{extended}_{t}(\tau)
is a territory and
\tau\mathrel{\triangleright_{t}}\mathit{extended}_{t}(\tau)
holds.

Analogous to the replaced territory, the above lemma would allow us to use
\mathit{extended}_{t}(\tau)
as the territory for the successor state of a state
\langle\tau,\varphi\rangle
,
whenever
\tau
is extendable by
t
.
While this would result in territories representing more than one marking,
it would still create more distinct territories (and thus states) than necessary.
The true power of extended territories is that they allow us to take shortcuts, and skip intermediate territories.

Lemma
5.7
.

If
\tau
is extendable with
t
, it holds that
\mathit{extended}_{t}(\tau)\mathrel{\triangleright_{t}}\mathit{extended}_{t}(\tau)
.
Moreover, if we have
\tau^{\prime}\mathrel{\triangleright_{t^{\prime}}}\tau
and
\mathit{bystanders}_{t^{\prime}}(\tau^{\prime})\subseteq\mathit{bystanders}_{t}(\tau)
,
then it also holds that
\tau^{\prime}\mathrel{\triangleright_{t^{\prime}}}\mathit{extended}_{t}(\tau)
.

\tau^{\prime}
\tau
\mathit{extended}_{t}(\tau)
t^{\prime}
t
t
t^{\prime}

Figure 8
.
Illustration of firing relationships implied by
Lemma

5.6
(dotted line) and
Lemma

5.7
(dashed lines).

Figure

8
illustrates the firing relationships implied by the lemmas.
The key take-away from this figure is that we do not necessarily need to construct a state with the territory
\tau
.
Instead, we can immediately construct a state with the territory
\mathit{extended}_{t}(\tau)
, and use this territory for the successor of
\tau^{\prime}
under transition
t^{\prime}
.
Moreover, if
\mathit{extended}_{t}(\tau)
in turn is extendable by yet another transition
t^{\prime\prime}
,
applying the same lemma again allows us (provided the bystander condition holds) to even use the territory
\mathit{extended}_{t^{\prime\prime}}(\mathit{extended}_{t}(\tau))
as the successor of
\tau^{\prime}
.
This process can be repeated over and over, as long as there are extending transitions that satisfy the bystander condition.

However, when using extended territories to construct an empire, we must be careful not to extend too much.
There are two limiting factors that can prevent us from extending with a transition.
First, as we have already seen, the bystander condition must hold in order for the extended territory to remain a possible successor for the territory
\tau^{\prime}
.
This is particularly relevant when the edge from
\tau^{\prime}
to
\tau
is not an extension.
Second, when we extend the territory for an empire state, thereby enlarging the set of markings it represents, we are claiming that the same law holds for all these markings.
To remain sound, we must be careful not to claim any law that is not proven by the given invariant domain
(A,\mathit{post})
.
Therefore, we only extend the territory with transitions that do not change the law.
The following definition captures these two limiting factors.

Definition 5.8.

Let
\tau
be a territory,
\varphi\in A
be a formula, and
\mathit{rb}
a set of regions.
The set of
saturated successors

\mathit{saturate}_{\varphi}^{rb}(\tau)

contains all territories
\tau^{\prime}
such that (recursively) one of the following holds:

•

\tau^{\prime}\in\mathit{saturate}_{\varphi}^{rb}(\mathit{extended}_{t}(\tau))
holds, for some transition
t
such that
\tau
is extendable by
t
, with
\lnot(\tau\mathrel{\triangleright_{t}}\tau)
,
\mathit{post}\left(\varphi,t\right)=\varphi
and
\mathit{rb}\subseteq\mathit{bystanders}_{t}(\tau)
; or

•

\tau=\tau^{\prime}
, and no such transition
t
exists.

Lemma
5.9
.

If
\tau\mathrel{\triangleright_{t}}\tau
and
\tau
is extendable with
t^{\prime}
, then
\mathit{extended}_{t^{\prime}}(\tau)\mathrel{\triangleright_{t}}\mathit{extended}_{t^{\prime}}(\tau)
.

Lemma
5.10
.

The set
\mathit{saturate}_{\varphi}^{\mathit{rb}}(\tau)
is always non-empty.
For all territories
\tau,\tau^{\prime}
and transitions
t
with
\tau\mathrel{\triangleright_{t}}\tau^{\prime}
and
\mathit{rb}=\mathit{bystanders}_{t}(\tau)
,
it holds that
\tau\mathrel{\triangleright_{t}}\tau^{\prime\prime}
for all
\tau^{\prime\prime}\in\mathit{saturate}_{\varphi}^{\mathit{rb}}(\tau^{\prime})
.

Typically, the set
\mathit{saturate}_{\varphi}^{rb}(\tau)
contains exactly one territory, i.e., there is a unique saturated successor,
as the order in which extending transitions
t
are applied does not matter.
The only exception are cases in which there is no clear separation of the places into threads,
namely, when two places
p_{1},p_{2}
can each be reached from a common predecessor place through only sequential transitions,
but
p_{1}
and
p_{2}
also occur together in a reachable marking (after a different sequence of transitions).
In our benchmarks in
Section

7
, the saturated successor is always unique.

With the notion of saturated successors, we have a condition to extend a given territory as much as possible.
Whenever our empire construction creates a new territory
\tau
,
it computes a saturated successor
\tau^{\prime}
of
\tau
and uses
\tau^{\prime}
in place of
\tau
.

Definition 5.11.

Given a Petri program
\mathcal{P}
and a safe invariant domain
(A,\mathit{post})
for
\mathcal{P}
,
a
saturated empire
is the reachable part of an empire
E=(Q,q_{\mathrm{init}},\delta,\mathit{law},\tau)
,
where

•

the states
Q\coloneq\mathbf{Terr}\times A
are pairs of territories and laws;

•

the initial state
q_{\mathrm{init}}\in\{\,\langle\tau,\top\rangle\mid\tau\in\mathit{saturate}_{\top}^{\emptyset}(\tau_{\mathrm{init}})\,\}

consists of a saturated successor of the territory
\tau_{\mathrm{init}}
and the law
\top
;

•

the transition function
\delta
satisfies

\delta(\langle\tau,\varphi\rangle,t)=\begin{cases}\text{undefined}&\textbf{if }t\not\in\mathit{enabled}(\tau)\text{ or }\mathit{post}\left(\varphi,t\right)\equiv\bot\\
\langle\tau,\varphi\rangle&\textbf{if }\tau\mathrel{\triangleright_{t}}\tau\text{ and }\mathit{post}\left(\varphi,t\right)\equiv\varphi\\
\langle\tau^{\prime},\varphi^{\prime}\rangle&\begin{aligned} \textbf{otherwise, }\text{where }&\varphi^{\prime}=\mathit{post}\left(\varphi,t\right),\ \mathit{rb}=\mathit{bystanders}_{t}(\tau)\\
&\text{ and }\tau^{\prime}\in\mathit{saturate}_{\varphi^{\prime}}^{\mathit{rb}}(\mathit{replaced}_{t}(\tau))\end{aligned}\end{cases}

•

and the law and territory mappings are given by
\mathit{law}(\langle\tau,\varphi\rangle)\coloneq\varphi
resp.
\mathit{terr}(\langle\tau,\varphi\rangle)\coloneq\tau
.

As before, we restrict to the reachable part in order to ensure that the validity condition
(safe
E
)
holds.
The following lemma shows that the validity conditions
(inductive-territory
E
)
and
(inductive-law
E
)
continue to hold for our improved transition function
\delta
.

Lemma
5.12
.

Let
\langle\tau,\varphi\rangle
be an empire state, and
t
a transition,
such that
\langle\tau^{\prime},\varphi^{\prime}\rangle\coloneq\delta(\langle\tau,\varphi\rangle,t)
is defined.
Then
\tau\mathrel{\triangleright_{t}}\tau^{\prime}
holds, and the Hoare triple
{\left\{\varphi\middle\}\;\lambda(t)\;\middle\{\varphi^{\prime}\right\}}
holds.

Theorem
5.13
.

Every saturated empire constructed from a safe invariant domain
(A,\mathit{post})
is valid.

The empire in
Fig.

5
is a saturated empire (i.e., it can be computed using our approach) for the program in
Fig.

2
and the invariant domain in
Example

3.10
.
In contrast to the naïve empire (
Fig.

7
), the saturated empire groups places and constructs larger regions, decreasing the number of states.

6.
Focus: Modular and Succinct Owicki-Gries Annotations

As discussed in
Section

4
,
imperial Owicki-Gries annotations still sometimes include unnecessary information.
For instance, in
Fig.

6
,
the annotations of the places
p_{5},p_{6},p_{7}
need not include any information about the variable
y
,
which can be thought of as a local variable of the left-hand thread.
So far, imperial Owicki-Gries annotations have no mechanism to split the law associated with an empire state and distribute this information among the places in the state’s territory.
Splitting this information allows us to construct more compact, succinct Owicki-Gries annotations.

Towards achieving such succinct Owicki-Gries annotations,
the idea of the
focus
is to make use of the modular structure inherent in the invariant domains computed by certain verification techniques.
Formally, this modular structure is captured by requiring that the invariant domain arises as a product of several smaller invariant domains.

Definition 6.1.

Let
(A_{1},\mathit{post}_{1}),\dots(A_{n},\mathit{post}_{n})
be invariant domains.
The
product invariant domain

(A,\mathit{post})=(A_{1},\mathit{post}_{1})\otimes\ldots\otimes(A_{n},\mathit{post}_{n})

consists of the invariants
A\coloneq\{\,\bigwedge_{i=1}^{n}\varphi_{i}\mid\varphi_{i}\in A_{i}\,\}\cup\{\bot,\top\}

and the function
\mathit{post}
with

\mathit{post}\left(\varphi,t\right)\coloneq\begin{cases}\bigwedge_{i=1}^{n}\mathit{post}_{i}\left(\top,t\right)&\textbf{if }\varphi=\top\\
\bigwedge_{i=1}^{n}\mathit{post}_{i}\left(\varphi_{i},t\right)&\textbf{if }\varphi=\bigwedge_{i=1}^{n}\varphi_{i}\text{ and }\mathit{post}_{i}\left(\varphi_{i},t\right)\neq\bot\text{ for all }i\\
\bot&\textbf{otherwise}\end{cases}

Product invariant domains arise from many different verification algorithms.
For instance in abstract interpretation, it is common to use product domains
(
Rival and Yi 2020
;
Cortesi et al. 2013
)
of multiple abstract domains running in parallel.
Applying an analysis with multiple abstract domains can be useful, as one abstract domain may be able to rule out certain program defects that the other can not, and vice versa.
From the fixpoint computed by the abstract interpretation engine then arises one invariant domain per abstract domain.
As another example, following a very different verification approach,
trace abstraction
-based verification algorithms
(
Heizmann et al. 2009
)
also produce product invariant domains,
by decomposing the set of a program’s error traces into multiple so-called
Floyd-Hoare automata
.
Each such automaton corresponds to one invariant domain.
Underlying both these approaches is the observation that the product of the computed invariant domains may be safe,
i.e., establish the program’s correctness,
even if none of the invariant domains on its own is safe.

The modularity of product invariant domains thus differs fundamentally from the notion of thread-modularity.
The individual invariant domains constitute separate proof arguments that
may prove specific sub-properties,
or establish the correctness of different program parts, i.e., subsets of interleavings (rather than particular threads).

Example 6.2.

As discussed in the introduction,
an algorithmic verifier might verify our example program (
Fig.

2
) by showing the two assert statements separately.
This yields two invariant domains
(A_{1},\mathit{post}_{1})
and
(A_{2},\mathit{post}_{2})
,
where

A_{1}=\{\,\top,\ y\leq x,\ y<x,\ \bot\,\}\quad\text{ resp.\ }\quad A_{2}=\{\,\top,\ x>0,\ x>1,\ x>2,\ \bot\,\}\ ,

and
\mathit{post}_{i}\left(\varphi,t\right)
is the strongest formula in
A_{i}
where
{\left\{\varphi\middle\}\;\lambda(t)\;\middle\{\mathit{post}_{i}\left(\varphi,t\right)\right\}}
holds, for
i=1,2
,
excepting that
\mathit{post}_{1}\left(y\leq x,\hbox to35.7pt{\vbox to11.19pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 17.8502pt\lower-2.84164pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} {{}{}{{
